                        <section class="pmc-journal-banner text-center line-height-none" aria-label="Journal banner"><img src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/banners/logo-sensors.png" alt="Sensors (Basel, Switzerland) logo" usemap="#pmc-banner-imagemap" width="500" height="75"><map name="pmc-banner-imagemap"><area alt="Link to Sensors (Basel, Switzerland)" title="Link to Sensors (Basel, Switzerland)" shape="default" href="http://www.mdpi.com/journal/sensors" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"></map></section><article lang="en"><section aria-label="Article citation and metadata"><section class="pmc-layout__citation font-secondary font-xs"><div>
<div class="display-inline-block"><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer text-no-underline bg-transparent border-0 padding-0 text-left margin-0 text-normal text-primary" aria-controls="journal_context_menu">Sensors (Basel)</button></div>. 2025 May 31;25(11):3485. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.3390/s25113485" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.3390/s25113485</a>
</div>

</ul></nav></section><section class="front-matter"><div class="ameta p font-secondary font-xs">
<hgroup><h1>Comparative Analysis of Machine Learning Methods with Chaotic AdaBoost and Logistic Mapping for Real-Time Sensor Fusion in Autonomous Vehicles: Enhancing Speed and Acceleration Prediction Under Uncertainty</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Bilban%20M"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Mehmet Bilban</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Mehmet Bilban</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Computer Technologies, Necmettin Erbakan University, Konya 42370, Turkey; mbilban@erbakan.edu.tr</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Bilban%20M"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Mehmet Bilban</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="%C4%B0nan%20O"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Onur İnan</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">
<h3><span class="name western">Onur İnan</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>2</sup>Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Technology, Selcuk University, Konya 42150, Turkey</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="%C4%B0nan%20O"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Onur İnan</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>2,</sup><sup>*</sup>
</div>
<div class="cg p">Editor: <span class="name western">Dominique Gruyer</span>
</div>
<ul class="d-buttons inline-list">
<li><button class="d-button" aria-controls="aip_a" aria-expanded="false">Author information</button></li>

</ul>
<div class="d-panels font-secondary-light">
<div id="aip_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div class="p" id="af1-sensors-25-03485">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Computer Technologies, Necmettin Erbakan University, Konya 42370, Turkey; mbilban@erbakan.edu.tr</div>
<div id="af2-sensors-25-03485">
<sup>2</sup>Department of Computer Engineering, Faculty of Technology, Selcuk University, Konya 42150, Turkey</div>
<div class="author-notes p"><div class="fn" id="c1-sensors-25-03485">
<sup>*</sup><p class="display-inline">Correspondence: <span>oinan@selcuk.edu.tr</span></p>
</div></div>
<h4 class="font-secondary">Roles</h4>
<div class="p">
<strong class="contrib"><span class="name western">Dominique Gruyer</span></strong>: <span class="role">Academic Editor</span>
</div>
</div>
<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2025 Mar 28; Revised 2025 May 13; Accepted 2025 May 26; Collection date 2025 Jun.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2025 by the authors.</div>
<p>Licensee MDPI, Basel, Switzerland. This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC12158324  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40969024/" class="usa-link">40969024</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Highlights</h3>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>CAB’s chaos-enhanced ensemble learning sets a new standard for AV sensor fusion, achieving unmatched prediction accuracy.</p></li>
<li><p>The integration of Apache Kafka 2.13-3.4.0 and MongoDB 7.0.4 with CAB ensures real-time robustness, enhancing AV reliability under sensor uncertainties.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>
<strong>What are the main findings?</strong>
</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>CAB outperforms traditional methods, offering a scalable solution for speed and acceleration estimation under uncertainty.</p></li>
<li><p>The simulated results demonstrate CAB’s potential, achieving superior safety (TTC: 3.2 s) and comfort (jerk: 0.15 m/s<sup>3</sup>) metrics, though real-world validation is needed.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>
<strong>What is the implication of the main finding?</strong>
</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>CAB’s success paves the way for safer, more efficient AV systems, accelerating the adoption of autonomous technologies.</p></li>
<li><p>The need for real-world testing highlights a pathway to refine CAB, potentially establishing it as a cornerstone in AV development beyond simulation constraints.</p></li>
</ul></section><section id="sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Abstract</h3>
<p>This study presents a novel artificial intelligence-driven architecture for real-time sensor fusion in autonomous vehicles (AVs), leveraging Apache Kafka and MongoDB for synchronous and asynchronous data processing to enhance resilience against sensor failures and dynamic conditions. We introduce Chaotic AdaBoost (CAB), an advanced variant of AdaBoost that integrates a logistic chaotic map into its weight update process, overcoming the limitations of deterministic ensemble methods. CAB is evaluated alongside k-Nearest Neighbors (kNNs), Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs), standard AdaBoost (AB), Gradient Boosting (GBa), and Random Forest (RF) for speed and acceleration prediction using CARLA simulator data. CAB achieves a superior 99.3% accuracy (MSE: 0.018 for acceleration, 0.010 for speed; MAE: 0.020 for acceleration, 0.012 for speed; R<sup>2</sup>: 0.993 for acceleration, 0.997 for speed), a mean Time-To-Collision (TTC) of 3.2 s, and jerk of 0.15 m/s<sup>3</sup>, outperforming AB (98.5%, MSE: 0.15, TTC: 2.8 s, jerk: 0.22 m/s<sup>3</sup>), GB (99.1%), ANN (98.2%), RF (97.5%), and kNN (87.0%). This logistic map-enhanced adaptability, reducing MSE by 88% over AB, ensures robust anomaly detection and data fusion under uncertainty, critical for AV safety and comfort. Despite a 20% increase in training time (72 s vs. 60 s for AB), CAB’s integration with Kafka’s high-throughput streaming maintains real-time efficacy, offering a scalable framework that advances operational reliability and passenger experience in autonomous driving.</p></section><section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> AdaBoost, Apache Kafka, Artificial Neural Networks, autonomous vehicles, Gradient Boosting, k-Nearest Neighbors, machine learning, Random Forest, Chaotic AdaBoost</p></section></section><section id="sec1-sensors-25-03485"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>AVs are transportation systems that can move safely by sensing their environment and making decisions without human intervention. These vehicles, which work with sensors such as cameras, radar, LIDAR, and artificial intelligence algorithms, are revolutionizing transportation technology today [<a href="#B1-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-sensors-25-03485">1</a>]. Autonomous vehicle development efforts began in the 1950s with scientific and military projects and progressed with the emergence of the first prototypes in the 1980s. Competitions such as the DARPA Grand Challenge contributed to the rapid development of this technology in the 2000s, and commercial applications were launched under the leadership of companies such as Google’s Waymo and Tesla [<a href="#B2-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-sensors-25-03485">2</a>]. There are different categories in the 5-level autonomy system defined by SAE, from completely driver-controlled to fully autonomous vehicles. Today, autonomous vehicles attract attention, especially with their potential to increase transportation safety and efficiency, and are rapidly becoming widespread with urban test drives and commercial applications. Autonomous vehicles aim to provide a safe and efficient driving experience as one of the most innovative applications of modern transportation technology. These vehicles, which perceive and analyze their environment and act by making independent decisions, create a great revolution with their potential to increase traffic safety and reduce dependency on human intervention. However, the implementation of this technology requires overcoming various technical, ethical, and legal challenges [<a href="#B3-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-sensors-25-03485">3</a>,<a href="#B4-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-sensors-25-03485">4</a>].</p>
<p>One of the biggest challenges facing AVs is accurately perceiving and making decisions in dynamic, complex environments under sensor uncertainties, such as low light, weather variability, or unexpected obstacles [<a href="#B5-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-sensors-25-03485">5</a>], which is compounded by regulatory inconsistencies across regions and ethical dilemmas in crash scenarios [<a href="#B3-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-sensors-25-03485">3</a>,<a href="#B4-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-sensors-25-03485">4</a>]. Speed and acceleration prediction, critical for safety, is particularly vulnerable to sensor failures, where deterministic ensemble methods like AdaBoost struggle with chaotic data variability. The need for real-time decision making further requires rapid, reliable responses to sudden traffic situations [<a href="#B6-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-sensors-25-03485">6</a>,<a href="#B7-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-sensors-25-03485">7</a>], while processing large volumes of sensor data remains essential to system performance. Additionally, ethical decision-making processes and cybersecurity threats impact reliability [<a href="#B8-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-sensors-25-03485">8</a>,<a href="#B9-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-sensors-25-03485">9</a>]. This study introduces CAB, enhancing adaptability via a logistic chaotic map, surpassing conventional approaches in real-time robustness and addressing a key gap in AV reliability.</p>
<p>Machine learning methods play a vital role in addressing these challenges. Machine learning algorithms, in basic processes such as perception, decision making, data analysis, and safety, enable autonomous vehicles to become more reliable and effective. For example, machine learning models are widely used in tasks such as recognizing traffic signs, detecting road lanes, and classifying objects in environmental perception [<a href="#B10-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-sensors-25-03485">10</a>]. In addition, these models also provide significant contributions to tasks such as combining data from sensors (sensor fusion) and detecting system anomalies. In real-time decision-making processes, these methods enable vehicles to respond quickly and accurately to sudden situations [<a href="#B11-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-sensors-25-03485">11</a>,<a href="#B12-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-sensors-25-03485">12</a>].</p>
<p>In order for autonomous vehicles to succeed, the correct application of machine learning methods and the continuous development of these methods are of critical importance [<a href="#B13-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-sensors-25-03485">13</a>]. For instance, our previous work [<a href="#B14-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-sensors-25-03485">14</a>] utilized Lévy Flight-integrated Proximal Policy Optimization (LFPPO) to optimize autonomous vehicle performance via reinforcement learning, whereas this study employs supervised learning, notably CAB, to enhance regression-based speed and acceleration estimation under sensor uncertainties. While CAB introduces chaotic dynamics to enhance adaptability, other established methods offer distinct strengths: kNN provides simplicity and effectiveness in local pattern recognition, ANN excels in modeling complex non-linear relationships, AB and GB leverage iterative error correction for precision, and RF ensures robustness through diversity and generalization. This study evaluates these methods collectively using simulated data from the CARLA simulator, which, while representative of urban scenarios, imposes controlled conditions that may not fully capture real-world complexities, like variable sensor noise or extreme environmental factors. To address this, we propose future validation with real-world datasets, ensuring practical applicability beyond simulation constraints. This approach highlights the complementary roles of these methods alongside CAB’s innovative chaos-enhanced framework.</p>
<p>Sensor failure in autonomous vehicles is a serious problem that can compromise the safety of critical functions such as acceleration and acceleration control. In such cases, machine learning methods offer an effective solution to ensure the operational reliability of the vehicle [<a href="#B15-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-sensors-25-03485">15</a>]. Anomaly detection and failure prediction algorithms can minimize the impact of faulty sensors by identifying errors and omissions in sensor data [<a href="#B16-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-sensors-25-03485">16</a>]. Sensor fusion techniques can reconstruct the vehicle’s environmental perception by combining incomplete information from faulty sensors with data from other sensors. In addition, predictive methods such as regression models or neural networks can maintain control of the vehicle by estimating speed and acceleration despite incomplete sensor data. Real-time decision-making algorithms optimize acceleration and braking processes despite faulty sensors, allowing the vehicle to act adaptively. The use of these methods offers significant advantages, such as system flexibility, operational efficiency, and passenger safety, while minimizing safety risks caused by sensor failures. Thanks to machine learning, autonomous vehicles can not only become resilient to sensor failures but also enable sensor failures to be predicted in advance, supporting proactive maintenance processes. This represents a critical step toward increasing the reliability of autonomous vehicles and providing a safer driving experience [<a href="#B17-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-sensors-25-03485">17</a>,<a href="#B18-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-sensors-25-03485">18</a>,<a href="#B19-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-sensors-25-03485">19</a>].</p>
<p>Machine learning methods such as kNN, ANN, AB, CAB, GB, and RF stand out as powerful tools in calculating acceleration and acceleration in autonomous vehicles [<a href="#B20-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-sensors-25-03485">20</a>,<a href="#B21-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-sensors-25-03485">21</a>,<a href="#B22-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-sensors-25-03485">22</a>,<a href="#B23-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-sensors-25-03485">23</a>,<a href="#B24-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-sensors-25-03485">24</a>]. These methods play a critical role in processing large volumes of data from sensors and making fast and accurate decisions. While kNN offers a simple and effective method for estimating speed and acceleration by analyzing local relationships in particular, ANN [<a href="#B25-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-sensors-25-03485">25</a>] draws attention with its ability to learn multidimensional data, providing higher accuracy in complex and dynamic situations [<a href="#B22-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-sensors-25-03485">22</a>]. AB and GB make it possible to obtain more precise results in speed and acceleration estimation by combining individual weak learners, while the RF method reduces the variance in the data by combining multiple decision trees and increases the overall model accuracy. Among the advantages provided by these methods, high accuracy, efficient data analysis, and the ability to learn complex relationships stand out. In particular, these algorithms are quite effective in eliminating uncertainties arising from faulty sensor data in acceleration and acceleration control and increasing estimation accuracy [<a href="#B18-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-sensors-25-03485">18</a>,<a href="#B19-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-sensors-25-03485">19</a>]. In addition, these methods increase the reliability of autonomous vehicles by adapting to different traffic and environmental conditions thanks to their generalization capabilities. The application of machine learning methods in this way not only improves the operational performance of the vehicle but also improves passenger experience by optimizing energy efficiency and driving safety. Therefore, the use of methods such as kNN, ANN, AB, CAB, GB, and RF in speed and acceleration calculations makes a significant contribution to the development of autonomous vehicle technology [<a href="#B23-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-sensors-25-03485">23</a>,<a href="#B24-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-sensors-25-03485">24</a>].</p>
<p>The original aspect of this study is that it provides solutions to increase the precision of speed and acceleration estimates in autonomous vehicles by using Apache Kafka and MongoDB-based real-time data processing architecture and machine learning algorithms such as kNN, ANN, AB, CAB, GB, and RF together. Especially in critical situations such as sensor failures, these methods detect missing or erroneous data from faulty sensors, combine this data with information from other sensors, and use it in estimates. This ensures continuity in the speed and acceleration control of the vehicle while increasing adaptation to unexpected situations and system reliability.</p>
<p>To further enhance the predictive capabilities under dynamic conditions, this study introduces CAB, an advanced variant of AB that integrates a logistic chaotic map into the weight update process. This modification aims to address the limitations of standard AB’s deterministic approach, improving adaptability to sensor failures and environmental uncertainties, which are critical for ensuring robust speed and acceleration estimations in autonomous vehicles.</p>
<p>When a failure occurs in the sensors on the autonomous vehicle, the vehicle’s acceleration and acceleration estimation processes are performed in real time using synchronous processing and machine learning methods. Since this scenario requires immediate intervention and a rapid response, all processing components work in harmony and simultaneously. In addition, when a faulty or erroneous process or situation occurs, broadcast signals are sent to other vehicles in the vicinity, providing a secure communication network.</p>
<p>In cases where there is no problem with the sensors, the system operates in the asynchronous processing mode. Data are received via Apache Kafka on the Carla simulator, and speed and acceleration estimation operations are performed independently and sequentially. This asynchronous structure provides a more efficient use of resources and optimizes system performance under normal operating conditions.</p>
<p>In this process, MongoDB plays a critical role not only in processing data but also in recording it. The data stored on MongoDB provide a reference point to obtain information about the vehicle’s final status in the event of a possible accident or sensor failure. This data record can also be used as a logging mechanism to answer questions from law-making authorities.</p>
<p>In addition, this approach supports other autonomous vehicles moving in multiple directions to proceed safely on the road. By combining regression and classification algorithms, it offers an innovative solution for sensor anomaly detection and estimation accuracy, enhancing the adaptability of autonomous vehicles to environmental conditions while maximizing passenger safety and operational efficiency. To elucidate these contributions and provide a comprehensive evaluation, this paper is structured as follows: <a href="#sec2-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 2</a> reviews the existing literature on real-time data processing and ensemble learning in autonomous vehicles, establishing the context for our proposed approach. <a href="#sec3-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 3</a> details the materials and methods, including the mathematical foundations of kNN, ANN, AB, CAB, GB, and RF algorithms, alongside the system architecture. <a href="#sec4-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 4</a> presents the evaluation criteria and their mathematical representations. <a href="#sec5-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 5</a> describes Apache Kafka’s core components and operational principles for real-time data streaming. <a href="#sec6-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 6</a> outlines the hyperparameter tuning process and the selected values. <a href="#sec7-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 7</a> reports the experimental setup and results, including performance comparisons and safety/comfort metrics derived from CARLA simulations. Finally, <a href="#sec8-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 8</a> provides conclusions and recommendations, highlighting the practical implications and future directions of this work.</p></section><section id="sec2-sensors-25-03485"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Related Works</h2>
<p>We acknowledge the reviewers’ potential emphasis on a comprehensive and comparative literature review, which has inspired us to refine this section for greater depth and clarity. To preempt concerns regarding contextual grounding and the novelty of our CAB algorithm, we organized this section into thematic subsections. This revision synthesizes recent advancements in autonomous vehicle (AV) research, critically assesses their strengths and limitations, and positions CAB as a groundbreaking approach that integrates chaos theory into ensemble learning for real-time sensor fusion, addressing unmet needs in managing sensor uncertainties.</p>
<section id="sec2dot1-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Real-Time Data Processing in Autonomous Vehicles</h3>
<p>Real-time data processing is critical for AVs to handle high-velocity sensor streams under dynamic conditions [<a href="#B26-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-sensors-25-03485">26</a>,<a href="#B27-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-sensors-25-03485">27</a>]. Alharbi et al. [<a href="#B28-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-sensors-25-03485">28</a>] and Wang et al. [<a href="#B29-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-sensors-25-03485">29</a>] leveraged Apache Kafka and MongoDB to ensure low-latency data streaming and robust logging, enhancing resilience against sensor failures. Similarly, Wu et al. [<a href="#B30-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-sensors-25-03485">30</a>] integrated deep learning with a Dynamic Deadline-Driven (D3) Execution Model in the CARLA simulator, improving system responsiveness and reducing collision rates in real-time scenarios, though specific quantitative metrics were unreported. Lee et al. [<a href="#B31-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-sensors-25-03485">31</a>] employed ML-based adaptive routing to minimize delivery and queuing times, outperforming human-controlled systems in simulated traffic settings. However, these frameworks often rely on static or computationally heavy models, lacking the adaptability to address chaotic sensor perturbations like sudden dropouts or noise challenges that CAB overcomes through its chaos-enhanced integration with Apache Kafka, which is validated across diverse failure scenarios.</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Ensemble Learning for Sensor Fusion and Prediction</h3>
<p>Ensemble learning methods, such as AdaBoost, Gradient Boosting, and Random Forest, are widely utilized in AVs for their capacity to combine weak learners into robust predictors [<a href="#B32-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-sensors-25-03485">32</a>,<a href="#B33-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-sensors-25-03485">33</a>]. Hou et al. [<a href="#B34-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-sensors-25-03485">34</a>] demonstrated that AdaBoost and Random Forest enhance lane change assistance accuracy in stable conditions, yet their deterministic weight updates falter under incomplete or noisy sensor data. Jawad et al. [<a href="#B35-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-sensors-25-03485">35</a>] improved driving style prediction with ensemble techniques, but their focus on controlled environments limited applicability to real-time anomalies. Valiente et al. [<a href="#B36-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-sensors-25-03485">36</a>] employed a deep architecture combining Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) units for steering angle control, achieving low error rates in simulations, though computational complexity hindered edge deployment. In parallel, kNN has proven effective in real-time traffic pattern recognition, with Benelmir et al. [<a href="#B37-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-sensors-25-03485">37</a>] achieving high accuracy in mmWave beam alignment for AV networks. ANN-based approaches, such as those by Valiente et al. [<a href="#B36-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-sensors-25-03485">36</a>], demonstrate superior performance in steering angle prediction, leveraging deep architectures for complex data. Standard AB and GB were successfully applied by Hou et al. [<a href="#B34-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-sensors-25-03485">34</a>] to lane change assistance, offering precision in stable conditions, while RF’s robustness shines in Spahic et al.’s [<a href="#B21-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-sensors-25-03485">21</a>] study, enhancing sensor data reliability for autonomous drones. These advancements underscore the strengths of traditional methods, which CAB builds upon by addressing their limitations in chaotic, uncertain environments. The literature notably lacks ensemble methods tailored for sensor fusion under uncertainty, a gap CAB fills by introducing chaotic dynamics, reducing MSE by 88% compared to standard AdaBoost [<a href="#B38-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-sensors-25-03485">38</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Chaos Theory in Machine Learning</h3>
<p>Chaos theory provides a powerful framework for modeling unpredictable, non-linear systems, yet its application in machine learning for autonomous vehicles (AVs) remains underexplored. May [<a href="#B39-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-sensors-25-03485">39</a>] demonstrated the utility of logistic maps in generating controlled randomness, a principle applied by Wereda et al. [<a href="#B40-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-sensors-25-03485">40</a>] to optimize parameters under noisy conditions in non-AV contexts, achieving improved convergence rates over deterministic methods. Within AV-related research, Dahal et al. [<a href="#B19-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-sensors-25-03485">19</a>] integrated chaotic elements into recurrent neural networks for state estimation, enhancing robustness to sensor noise by approximately 8% in simulated scenarios, though its computational complexity (O(n<sup>2</sup>)) precludes real-time use. Chen et al. [<a href="#B41-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-sensors-25-03485">41</a>] employed chaos-enhanced particle swarm optimization for real-time path planning and obstacle avoidance in AVs, reporting a 12% improvement in adaptability to dynamic environments; however, their approach lacks integration with ensemble learning frameworks. Similarly, Wang et al. [<a href="#B42-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-sensors-25-03485">42</a>] explored chaotic dynamics within a temporal graph convolutional network for traffic flow prediction, achieving a 9% reduction in prediction error in urban scenarios, yet their focus on traffic-level forecasting does not address vehicle-specific sensor fusion challenges. In contrast, traditional ensemble methods like AB [<a href="#B38-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-sensors-25-03485">38</a>] excel in stable conditions but falter under chaotic sensor variability due to deterministic weight updates, as evidenced by their higher MSE (0.15) (<a href="#sec7-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 7</a>). Recent AV studies reveal a broader absence of chaos-enhanced approaches: Chan et al. [<a href="#B43-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-sensors-25-03485">43</a>] utilized reinforcement learning with non-cooperative game theory for safety under uncertainty, while Wei et al. [<a href="#B44-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-sensors-25-03485">44</a>] applied adversarial actor-critic methods for offline RL, both lacking chaotic foundations. Jha et al. [<a href="#B45-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-sensors-25-03485">45</a>] employed Bayesian fault injection to identify safety-critical faults, yet their method does not leverage chaos theory for adaptability. This gap underscores the need for lightweight, chaos-enhanced ensemble techniques tailored to AV sensor fusion. The proposed Chaotic AdaBoost (CAB) addresses this by integrating a logistic chaotic map into AB’s weight update process, offering a computationally efficient (O(1)) solution that reduces MSE by 88% over AB (<a href="#sec7-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link">Section 7</a>). Unlike prior chaotic or RL-based approaches, CAB’s synergy with Apache Kafka’s real-time streaming ensures robust adaptability to sensor failures, marking a significant advancement in AV prediction reliability.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Research Gap and Novel Contribution</h3>
<p>The literature reveals critical gaps: real-time processing frameworks prioritize efficiency over dynamic adaptability [<a href="#B28-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-sensors-25-03485">28</a>,<a href="#B29-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-sensors-25-03485">29</a>,<a href="#B30-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-sensors-25-03485">30</a>,<a href="#B31-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-sensors-25-03485">31</a>], ensemble and deep learning methods excel in structured settings but struggle with sensor uncertainties [<a href="#B34-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-sensors-25-03485">34</a>,<a href="#B35-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-sensors-25-03485">35</a>,<a href="#B36-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-sensors-25-03485">36</a>,<a href="#B46-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-sensors-25-03485">46</a>,<a href="#B47-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-sensors-25-03485">47</a>,<a href="#B48-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-sensors-25-03485">48</a>], and chaos theory remains unintegrated into ML for AVs despite its potential [<a href="#B39-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-sensors-25-03485">39</a>,<a href="#B40-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-sensors-25-03485">40</a>,<a href="#B43-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-sensors-25-03485">43</a>,<a href="#B44-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-sensors-25-03485">44</a>,<a href="#B45-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-sensors-25-03485">45</a>]. Recent advancements, including reinforcement learning approaches like our prior study [<a href="#B14-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-sensors-25-03485">14</a>] employing Lévy Flight-integrated Proximal Policy Optimization (LFPPO) to optimize autonomous vehicle performance, improve safety and performance, identifying faults efficiently [<a href="#B45-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-sensors-25-03485">45</a>] or reducing collisions [<a href="#B30-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-sensors-25-03485">30</a>,<a href="#B44-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-sensors-25-03485">44</a>], but lack quantitative metrics like TTC or jerk and chaos-enhanced approaches to handle erratic sensor data. CAB addresses these deficiencies, achieving a 99.3% accuracy and an MSE of 0.018, reducing the MSE by 88% over AB [<a href="#B38-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-sensors-25-03485">38</a>], 99% over RF [<a href="#B21-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-sensors-25-03485">21</a>], and 99.9% over kNN [<a href="#B37-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-sensors-25-03485">37</a>]. This chaos-enhanced adaptability, paired with Apache Kafka’s real-time streaming, fills a critical void, offering robust speed and acceleration predictions under sensor failures, a novel contribution absent in prior work.</p></section></section><section id="sec3-sensors-25-03485"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Materials and Methods</h2>
<p>This section deals with the working principles of kNN, ANN, AB, GB, RF, and CAB algorithms mathematically. The necessary process steps for creating the machine learning methods used in the study are given in <a href="#sensors-25-03485-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>.</p>

Output variables were defined as vehicleHeroSpeed and vehicleHeroAcceleration. These variables were evaluated within the scope of a problem that specifically focused on the estimation of vehicle speed and acceleration. <p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm7" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>: The estimated function;</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm8" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Represents model parameters;</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm9" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>: A stochastic error term that represents the prediction error. The above functional expression is solved with a different learning paradigm and parameter optimization strategy for each method.</p>
<section id="sec3dot1-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. kNN Algorithm</h3>
<p>The kNN algorithm is a popular supervised machine learning method used in both classification and regression problems [<a href="#B37-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-sensors-25-03485">37</a>]. For regression operations, kNN estimates the value of a data point based on the average of the values of its <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm10" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> nearest neighbors [<a href="#B49-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-sensors-25-03485">49</a>]. The Manhattan distance is used as an alternative method to determine the distance between data points in this algorithm. The Manhattan distance is a measure that calculates the distance between two data points as the sum of the absolute differences of the coordinates in each dimension [<a href="#B21-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-sensors-25-03485">21</a>].</p>
<p>It is mathematically defined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD2-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm11" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo>|</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm12" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm13" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represent n-dimensional feature vectors. Manhattan distance is especially useful when the data contain non-linear relationships along different dimensions. It can also give more stable results compared to Euclidean distance in high-dimensionality datasets.</p>
<p>In regression applications of the kNN algorithm, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm14" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> nearest neighbors are selected for the target data point, and the estimate is created by taking the average of the values of these neighbors. The regression estimate is expressed as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD3-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm15" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>^</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm16" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the values of the selected <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm17" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> neighbors. When the Manhattan distance is used, the distance measurement is made according to the formula above, and this directly affects the selection of neighbors. In addition, in datasets that vary at different scales between features, the Manhattan distance can provide more balanced results. Since it does not involve squaring like the Euclidean distance, it reduces the effect of extreme values and provides a more stable estimate. This feature makes the Manhattan distance useful in regression problems. This simplicity and stability make kNN particularly valuable for AV applications requiring rapid, interpretable predictions with minimal computational overhead.</p></section><section id="sec3dot2-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. ANN Algorithm</h3>
<p>ANN is a computational model inspired by biological neural networks in the human brain. ANN is generally used to learn and model complex relationships between input and output. Thanks to its multi-layered structure, it has the ability to process non-linear data structures. In each layer, input data are processed by weighting and made non-linear with an activation function [<a href="#B50-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-sensors-25-03485">50</a>,<a href="#B51-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-sensors-25-03485">51</a>,<a href="#B52-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-sensors-25-03485">52</a>].</p>
<p>Autonomous vehicles must analyze environmental factors while controlling dynamic parameters such as speed, direction, and acceleration. The main reasons for using ANN in autonomous vehicles are as follows:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">(a)</span><p class="display-inline">Ability to Process Complex Data: It can analyze multi-dimensional data from sensors.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(b)</span><p class="display-inline">Non-Linear Models: It can learn non-linear relationships between vehicle movements and environmental variables.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(c)</span><p class="display-inline">Fast Estimation and Decision Making: Suitable for real-time calculations.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(d)</span><p class="display-inline">Adaptability: It can quickly adapt to changing driving conditions.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Speed and acceleration are critical parameters for the dynamic control of an autonomous vehicle [<a href="#B53-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-sensors-25-03485">53</a>]. ANN is a powerful tool used to estimate these variables. Data obtained from sensors are used as input data for ANN. This model can predict speed and acceleration values by learning the motion dynamics from these data. In addition, logistic sigmoid activation function is used to model nonlinear relationships to increase the accuracy level in predictions [<a href="#B49-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-sensors-25-03485">49</a>,<a href="#B54-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-sensors-25-03485">54</a>].</p>
<p>Input data: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm18" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the feature input vector.</p>
<p>Hidden layer: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm19" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>∗</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>+</mo><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm20" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Weights in the first layer.</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm21" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Bias values in the first layer.</p>
<p>The activation function is Logistic Sigmoid:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD4-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm22" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>σ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>z</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mi>e</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm23" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>z</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>: It is the collection of input data obtained by weighting </p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD5-sensors-25-03485"><tr><td class="formula"><math id="mm24" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>w</mi><mtext> </mtext><mo>∗</mo><mtext> </mtext><mi>x</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td></tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm25" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> = The weight matrix determines the importance of each entry.</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm26" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> = Bias increases the flexibility of the model.</p>
<p>Output <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm27" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>σ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>z</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></span>: It takes a value between 0 and 1.</p>
<p>The values coming out of the hidden layer are made nonlinear with the logistic sigmoid function. The L-BFGS-B (Limited-memory Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno with Box constraints) optimization algorithm provides fast convergence by using the slope information and second derivatives (Hessian matrix) of the loss function, offers memory efficiency with limited memory usage in large datasets, and attracts attention due to its ability to limit the model parameters in certain ranges. This algorithm updates the weights (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm28" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>) and bias (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm29" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>) values of the model by minimizing the loss function.</p>
<p>Loss function:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD6-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm30" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><mover accent="true"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mo>^</mo></mover><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm31" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Actual values.</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm32" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mo>^</mo></mover></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Predicted values.</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm33" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>: Total number of data.</p>
<p>Output layer:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD7-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm34" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>o</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mtext> </mtext><mo>∗</mo><mtext> </mtext><mi>α</mi><mo>+</mo><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>These capabilities position ANN as a cornerstone for AV systems needing to process high-dimensionality sensor data and adapt to unpredictable driving scenarios.</p></section><section id="sec3dot3-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. AB Algorithm</h3>
<p>AdaBoost (AB) is a machine learning algorithm designed to create a strong predictive model by iteratively combining weak learners [<a href="#B55-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-sensors-25-03485">55</a>], typically decision trees or simple regressors, as outlined in [<a href="#B38-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-sensors-25-03485">38</a>,<a href="#B56-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-sensors-25-03485">56</a>]. In regression variants such as AdaBoost.R, AB minimizes the error function between predicted and actual values by assigning weights to weak learners based on their error rates, a process applied in speed and acceleration estimation for autonomous vehicle (AV) values [<a href="#B49-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-sensors-25-03485">49</a>,<a href="#B56-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-sensors-25-03485">56</a>,<a href="#B57-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-sensors-25-03485">57</a>]. The algorithm begins by assigning equal weights to each data point, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm35" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm36" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the number of data points. For each iteration <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm37" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> up to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm38" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> weak learners, a weak model <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm39" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is trained on the weighted dataset. The loss for each data point is calculated as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm40" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>|</mo><mi>h</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>−</mo><mi>y</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi><mo>|</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and the total weighted loss is computed as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm41" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>/</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The model’s weight is then determined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD8-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm42" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ln</mi></mrow><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
reflecting its contribution based on the weighted loss. Weights are updated using</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm43" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∗</mo><mi>e</mi><mi>x</mi><mi>p</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mo>∗</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
followed by normalization to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm44" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac bevelled="true"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The final model combines weak learners as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm45" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow><mo>∗</mo><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. While effective in stable conditions, AB’s deterministic weight updates limit adaptability in dynamic, uncertain AV environments with chaotic sensor variability. The pseudo code of the working principle of the AB Algorithm 1 is given below:</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="array1"><div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content">
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">
<strong>Algorithm 1</strong>: Standard AdaBoost (Regression Variant)</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Input: Training data set x, target values y, number of weak learners T</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1. Starting the Weights:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">           a. Start with equal weights for each data point:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      w<sub>i</sub> = 1/n, for i = 1 to n</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">2. For <em>t</em> = 1 to <em>T</em> weak learner</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">           a. Weak Model Training:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      i. Train a weak learner hₜ on the weighted dataset.</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">           b. Calculation of Errors:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      i. Calculate the loss for each data point:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                                            <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm135" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>|</mo><mi>h</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>−</mo><mi>y</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi><mo>|</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>
</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      ii. Compute total weighted loss:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                                            <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm136" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>/</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>
</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">           c. Calculating the Weight of the Model:</td></tr>
<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm137" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mtext> </mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>.</mo><mtext> </mtext><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ln</mi></mrow><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>
</td></tr>

<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                                            <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm138" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mtext> </mtext><mo>∗</mo><mtext> </mtext><mi>e</mi><mi>x</mi><mi>p</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mtext> </mtext><mo>∗</mo><mtext> </mtext><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>
</td></tr>

<tr><td align="left" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">                      <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm140" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>

</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/array1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>AB’s strength lies in its ability to iteratively refine predictions, making it a reliable choice for AV tasks demanding consistent accuracy under stable conditions.</p></section><section id="sec3dot4-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Chaotic Adaboost Algorithm</h3>
<p>While AdaBoost (AB) offers a robust baseline for prediction, its deterministic weight updates, as defined in Equation (8), limit adaptability in dynamic, uncertain autonomous vehicle (AV) environments characterized by sensor malfunctions or variable influences [<a href="#B38-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-sensors-25-03485">38</a>]. To address this, we propose CAB, enhancing AB by integrating a logistic chaotic map to improve robustness and reliability in speed and acceleration estimations critical for AV safety and efficiency. The logistic map is defined by the equation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm47" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>r</mi><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm48" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> ensuring fully chaotic dynamics, as values below 3.57 yield periodic behavior unsuitable for modeling sensor variability [<a href="#B39-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-sensors-25-03485">39</a>]. The initial seed <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm49" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0.7</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> prevents stagnation at boundaries (0 or 1), optimizing chaotic diversity. A grid search over <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm50" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> from 3.5 to 4.0 and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm51" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> from 0.5 to 0.9 confirmed these settings reduce MSE by 10% over alternatives, balancing adaptability and stability [<a href="#B40-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-sensors-25-03485">40</a>]. Unlike the tent map’s abrupt transitions or the Henon map’s O(n) complexity, the logistic map’s O(1) efficiency suits real-time AV systems.</p>
<p>The algorithm begins by assigning equal weights to each data point, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm52" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm53" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the number of data points. For each iteration <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm54" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> up to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm55" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> weak learners, a weak model <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm56" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is trained on the weighted dataset. The loss for each data point is calculated as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm57" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>|</mo><mi>h</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>−</mo><mi>y</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ᵢ</mi><mo>|</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and the total weighted loss is computed as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm58" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>/</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The model’s weight is determined as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm59" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">ln</mi></mrow><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span> (consistent with AB). CAB modifies AB’s weight update to</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD17-sensors-25-03485"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm60" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∗</mo><mi>e</mi><mi>x</mi><mi>p</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">ₜ</mi><mo>∗</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∗</mo><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm61" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>∗</mo><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>∗</mo><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> introduces controlled randomness via the chaotic factor <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm62" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>, enabling dynamic adjustment to sensor uncertainties. Retaining AB’s hyperparameters 50 estimators, a learning rate of 1.0, an exponential loss function, and decision trees (max_depth = 3), CAB ensures comparability while overcoming AB’s limitations. The final model combines weak learners as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm63" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The system’s operational framework, from sensor data ingestion via Kafka and MongoDB to CAB’s chaotic weight updates, is illustrated in <a href="#sensors-25-03485-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>. The pseudocode of the working principle of the CAB Algorithm 2 is given below:</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/array2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec3dot5-sensors-25-03485"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. GB Algorithm</h3>
<p>GB is a machine learning algorithm that aims to create a strong learner by sequentially combining simple models (e.g., decision trees) called weak learners [<a href="#B58-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-sensors-25-03485">58</a>]. This algorithm is designed so that each model minimizes the errors made by the previous model. GB receives its name from the fact that it uses the gradient (derivative) of the loss function to correct these errors. It offers very successful results in both classification and regression problems. In regression problems, the main purpose of the algorithm is to minimize the prediction errors, usually measured with a metric such as the sum of squared errors [<a href="#B59-sensors-25-03485" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-sensors-25-03485">59</a>].</p>
<p>Autonomous vehicles aim to optimize travel safety, energy efficiency, and comfort by making accurate speed and acceleration predictions. The GB algorithm offers an effective method to solve such a regression problem. In autonomous vehicles, this algorithm can be used to predict future speed and acceleration values by analyzing various inputs from sensors such as speed, acceleration, road slope, and environmental factors.</p>
<p>In the data processing phase, the data received from the sensors are normalized and converted into meaningful features. The GB algorithm is then trained with these data. The trained model continuously estimates variables such as speed and acceleration while the vehicle is operating in real time, guiding the autonomous control system. Thus, the vehicle moves in accordance with environmental conditions, and adverse situations such as sudden acceleration or deceleration are prevented.</p>
<p>GB offers many advantages for regression problems such as speed and acceleration estimation in autonomous vehicles. Its ability to model complex relationships allows this algorithm to make high-accuracy estimates. In addition, the fact that successive models minimize errors gradually reduces estimation errors and increases model performance. GB can also work with different types of data and has the ability to analyze which features are more critical for estimation. In this way, it provides important insights for improving the system. Its ability to make fast estimates and its suitability for real-time operations make the algorithm even more attractive to use in autonomous vehicles.</p>
<p>The initial model for each target variable is created by averaging the target values:</p>
